Hi, Dr. ____. Nice to meet you. Um, well, I guess I came in because I'm just having a really hard time keeping on track with my grading. I teach high school, so I'll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work. 

Actually, the essays right now are on *The Great Gatsby*, and it is wild how the kids interpret it these days. Like, when I was their age, everyone thought Gatsby was this tragic romantic, but my students are totally zeroed in on the socioeconomic stuff. I think TikTok has actually changed how they analyze literature, which is fascinating, honestly. I saw this one video the other day that was breaking down the color symbolism in a way I hadn't even thought about since college... 

Wait, sorry, what was I talking about? Oh, right, my grading. So yeah, I sit down with the essays, ready to go, and within like ten minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. I'll be thinking about three other completely different things—like that video, or what I need to get at the grocery store—and I'm barely taking in a single word of what’s right in front of me.

Yeah, definitely. It's always been a thing. Even back in primary school, my report cards were basically a broken record. They always said stuff like, uh, "bright but careless" or that I wouldn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised.

Oh, yeah, definitely. All the time. I forget ordinary responsibilities way more than I want to admit. Like, I'll completely blank on replying to a parent email, or I'll forget to pay a bill until the actual late notice shows up in the mail. 

Actually, speaking of the mail, our mail carrier is this really nice guy named Frank, and he always brings treats for the neighborhood dogs. My wife and I don't even have a dog, but we've been talking about getting one lately. She really wants a Golden Retriever, but they shed so much, and honestly, our vacuum is already making this awful rattling noise. I tried to take it apart last weekend to see if something was stuck in the brush, but I couldn't find my good screwdriver set. I think I might have left it over at my brother's house when I helped him put together his patio furniture last month. He bought this huge set from IKEA, and it took us like six hours...

Wait, sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Oh, right, forgetting things. Yeah, so emails, bills... and just simple stuff, too. Like, I'll pull into the driveway and realize I completely forgot to pick up whatever my wife asked me to grab from the store on the way home. I'll have driven right past the grocery store without it even crossing my mind.

Um, worrying? Not really, no. I mean, I worry about normal stuff, like if I'm going to get my grades entered on time, but I wouldn't say I worry a lot every day. 

Actually, speaking of grades, our school just switched to this new online grading portal, and it is so confusing. The interface is completely different from the old one, and the colors are really weird. It's like this bright orange and blue, which are our rival school's colors, so everyone in the staff lounge was complaining about it. The principal said it was cheaper, but you'd think they could at least customize the colors, right? I was trying to figure out how to input a late assignment yesterday, and I ended up clicking on this link that took me to a totally different page about state standards. Which is a whole other thing, because they keep changing the standards every few years, and we have to rewrite all our lesson plans...

Wait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm a big worrier. Just regular stuff.

Yeah, actually. I mean, as far back as I can remember, really. Even in primary school, my report cards always said the exact same things, like, "bright but careless" or "doesn't finish work unless closely supervised." I had this one that was Ninja Turtles—Donatello, specifically, because he was the smart one with the bo staff. Interrupt? Um, no, I don't think I do that. I mean, I try really hard to be a good listener. It's super important in teaching, obviously. You have to let the kids finish their thoughts, even if it takes them forever to get to the point. 

Which, man, sometimes they really do take forever. Especially the younger ones. I had this one kid, Tyler, a couple years ago, who would raise his hand and then just start telling me about his weekend, completely unrelated to the lesson. Like, we'd be talking about the French Revolution, and suddenly he's giving me a play-by-play of his cousin's birthday party at this trampoline park. Have you ever been to one of those places? They're actually kind of terrifying. It's just wall-to-wall trampolines and foam pits, and kids are just launching themselves everywhere. I went to one for my nephew's birthday last year, and I swear I almost blew out my knee just trying to bounce normally. The foam pits are the worst part, though. You jump in, and it's like quicksand. It took me a solid five minutes to claw my way out, and I felt so ridiculous. Plus, you just know those foam blocks are absolutely covered in germs. I read this article once about how many bacteria they found in a ball pit at a fast-food restaurant, and it was... 

Wait. Sorry. Interrupting. Right. No, I don't really interrupt people. I usually just let them talk.

Yeah, it’s definitely everywhere. At home, my wife has basically started taking over all the bills and reminders because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. I mean, I get frustrated with myself for forgetting things all the time, but not, like, sad. \n\nMy wife actually asked me something similar the other day because I was staring into space while we were eating dinner. We were having this amazing lasagna she makes—it has, like, three different kinds of cheese in it. Ricotta, mozzarella, and I think parmesan? Or maybe it was provolone. Anyway, she uses this specific brand of crushed tomatoes that we can only find at the Italian deli across town. We usually try to go on Saturday mornings, but parking over there is an absolute nightmare. They have this tiny little lot, and people just kind of abandon their cars wherever. Last time we went, this guy in a giant pickup truck completely blocked us in, and we had to wait like twenty minutes for him to come out with his sandwiches...\n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking about feeling down, right? Yeah, no, I'm not depressed. Just... easily distracted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot most days?\n\nPatient: Um, worrying? Not really, no. I mean, I worry about normal stuff, like if I'm going to get my grades entered on time, but I wouldn't say I worry a lot every day. \n\nActually, speaking of grades, our school just switched to this new online grading portal, and it is so confusing. The interface is completely different from the old one, and the colors are really weird. It's like this bright orange and blue, which are our rival school's colors, so everyone in the staff lounge was complaining about it. The principal said it was cheaper, but you'd think they could at least customize the colors, right? I was trying to figure out how to input a late assignment yesterday, and I ended up clicking on this link that took me to a totally different page about state standards. Which is a whole other thing, because they keep changing the standards every few years, and we have to rewrite all our lesson plans...\n\nWait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm a big worrier. Just regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you already have these same attention and forgetfulness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. I mean, as far back as I can remember, really. Even in primary school, my report cards always said the exact same things, like, \"bright but careless\" or \"doesn't finish work unless closely supervised.\" My parents were constantly finding these crumpled up worksheets stuffed at the bottom of my backpack that I completely forgot to turn in. \n\nWhich, honestly, backpacks these days are so much better designed. Have you seen the ones my students carry? They have all these specific padded compartments for laptops and tablets, and little mesh pockets for water bottles. When I was a kid, we just had those basic canvas ones with one big pocket, and everything just sank to the bottom into this black hole. I had this one that was Ninja Turtles—Donatello, specifically, because he was the smart one with the bo staff. I actually tried to make a bo staff out of a broom handle once in the backyard, and I ended up whacking my mom's prized rose bushes. She was so mad. She used to spend hours on her knees in the garden every Sunday, wearing these huge floppy sun hats... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, the forgetfulness and not finishing things, that's been happening since I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt unusually energetic or driven?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I definitely need my sleep. If I don't get at least seven hours, I'm basically a zombie the next day at school. \n\nAlthough, honestly, getting good sleep has been tough lately because my neighbor just got this new dog. It's some kind of husky mix, I think? And it just howls at all hours of the night. It's not even a bark, it's this long, drawn-out wail, like a wolf. I read somewhere that huskies are super vocal because they were bred to pull sleds and had to communicate with each other over long distances in the snow. Which is fascinating, really. I actually showed my history class this documentary about the Iditarod a few years ago when we were learning about Alaska. Did you know they use these special little booties for the dogs' paws so they don't get cut up on the ice? They're usually bright pink or neon green, which looks so funny against the snow. One of my students actually tried to knit booties for her cat after watching it, but she said the cat just kicked them off immediately and hid under the sofa for two days...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Energy? Yeah, no, I've never had a time where I didn't need sleep and felt super energetic. I'm usually just tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving when you’re supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. I mean, I can sit through a faculty meeting or a movie just fine without feeling like I need to jump out of my chair or pace around or anything. \n\nThough, speaking of movies, I finally went to see that new sci-fi epic everyone's been talking about. The cinematography was incredible, especially the scenes on that desert planet. They actually filmed a lot of it in Jordan, in Wadi Rum, which is the same place they shot Lawrence of Arabia. I've always wanted to visit Jordan. The architecture in Petra is just mind-blowing, how they carved an entire city directly into the rose-red sandstone cliffs. I was telling my students about it when we were covering ancient trade routes, because the Nabataeans who built it got super wealthy from controlling the incense trade. Frankincense and myrrh, mostly. It's crazy to think about how valuable tree resin used to be, right? Like, wars were fought over sap. I actually bought some frankincense essential oil once to see what the big deal was, but it just made my apartment smell like an old church, so I ended up giving it to my mom...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What was the question? Oh, sitting still. Yeah, no, I don't get restless or feel like I have to keep moving. I'm pretty okay just sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Interrupt? Um, no, I don't think I do that. I mean, I try really hard to be a good listener. It's super important in teaching, obviously. I had this one kid, Tyler, a couple years ago, who would raise his hand and then just start telling me about his weekend, completely unrelated to the lesson. Like, we'd be talking about the French Revolution, and suddenly he's giving me a play-by-play of his cousin's birthday party at this trampoline park. Have you ever been to one of those places? They're actually kind of terrifying. It's just wall-to-wall trampolines and foam pits, and kids are just launching themselves everywhere. I went to one for my nephew's birthday last year, and I swear I almost blew out my knee just trying to bounce normally. The foam pits are the worst part, though. You jump in, and it's like quicksand. It took me a solid five minutes to claw my way out, and I felt so ridiculous. Plus, you just know those foam blocks are absolutely covered in germs. I read this article once about how many bacteria they found in a ball pit at a fast-food restaurant, and it was... \n\nWait. Sorry. Interrupting. Right. No, I don't really interrupt people. I usually just let them talk.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and forgetfulness problems cause difficulties for you in more than one setting, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely everywhere. At home, my wife has basically started taking over all the bills and reminders because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. And at work, the missed follow-through has actually become pretty serious. My department chair just put me on a formal support plan. \n\nShe called me into her office to give me the paperwork, and she had it all organized in this blindingly bright yellow folder. Like, blue is supposed to be super calming and helps you focus, which is why a lot of corporate offices use it. But yellow is actually known to cause anxiety if you're exposed to too much of it. It's why fast-food places use so much yellow and red—it supposedly makes you hungry but also makes you feel rushed so you eat fast and leave. McDonald's, Burger King, they all do it. Which is just a weird name for paint, right? Like, who wants their walls to remind them of dairy? I don't even really eat butter anymore since I've been trying to watch my cholesterol. I switched to that olive oil spread, but it just doesn't melt the same way on a toasted bagel...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm rambling again. What were you asking? Reading and math were actually fine for me, I understood the material. It was definitely just staying focused and actually finishing the work. Even back in primary school, my report cards always said things like, "bright but careless," or "doesn't finish work unless closely supervised." And my parents were constantly finding all these half-finished worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. 

Backpacks were so different back then, though. I had this really cool Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles one in second grade. It was one of those plastic-y ones that cracked if it got too cold? It's wild how something created as a total joke became this massive billion-dollar franchise with toys and movies and everything. I actually found some of my old action figures in my parents' attic a few months ago. The plastic gets kind of sticky when it gets old, which is super gross. It's called polymer degradation, I think. The plasticizers they use to make the plastic flexible start to seep out over time. It's a huge problem for museums trying to preserve modern artifacts, like old spacesuits from the Apollo missions. They're literally just melting away in storage because nobody knows how to stop the chemical breakdown...

Oh, wait. Sorry. I'm doing it again. Um, but yeah, the school stuff. The subjects themselves weren't the problem, it was just the focusing and actually getting the work done and turned in.

Like a parody of all the gritty comic books that were popular in the 80s, like Daredevil and Ronin. It's wild how something created as a total joke became this massive billion-dollar franchise with toys and movies and everything. I actually found some of my old action figures in my parents' attic a few months ago. The plastic gets kind of sticky when it gets old, which is super gross. It's called polymer degradation, I think. The plasticizers they use to make the plastic flexible start to seep out over time. It's a huge problem for museums trying to preserve modern artifacts, like old spacesuits from the Apollo missions. They're literally just melting away in storage because nobody knows how to stop the chemical breakdown...\n\nOh, wait. Sorry. I'm doing it again. Um, but yeah, the school stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no trouble learning them. Like I was just saying, I understood the material fine, it was just the focusing part that was hard. I actually really liked reading when I was a kid, especially those Choose Your Own Adventure books? 

Man, I loved those. You know, where it says "If you want to open the door, turn to page 42. If you want to run away, turn to page 73." I used to keep all my fingers tucked into the pages so I could go back and change my choice if I died. Which kind of defeats the purpose of the book, I guess, but I hated getting a bad ending. I think the guy who invented those books was actually a lawyer or something, and he came up with the idea while telling bedtime stories to his daughters. He'd ask them what the characters should do next. It's such a brilliant concept, making the reader part of the narrative. They've kind of tried to do that with video games now, with all these branching storylines and multiple endings, but it's not quite the same feeling as physically flipping the pages. I tried to get some of my students into those interactive fiction games online, like Twine games? But they just don't have the patience for all that text without any graphics. Kids today are so used to high-definition everything, they don't want to use their imagination to picture the scene...

Sorry. I'm doing it again. But no, to answer your question, no trouble learning to read or do math. Just the staying on task part.

We were having this amazing lasagna she makes—it has, like, three different kinds of cheese in it. Ricotta, mozzarella, and I think parmesan? Or maybe it was provolone. Anyway, she uses this specific brand of crushed tomatoes that we can only find at the Italian deli across town. We usually try to go on Saturday mornings, but parking over there is an absolute nightmare. They have this tiny little lot, and people just kind of abandon their cars wherever. Last time we went, this guy in a giant pickup truck completely blocked us in, and we had to wait like twenty minutes for him to come out with his sandwiches...\n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking about feeling down, right? Yeah, no, I'm not depressed. Just... easily distracted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot most days?\n\nPatient: Um, worrying? Not really, no. I mean, I worry about normal stuff, like if I'm going to get my grades entered on time, but I wouldn't say I worry a lot every day. \n\nActually, speaking of grades, our school just switched to this new online grading portal, and it is so confusing. The interface is completely different from the old one, and the colors are really weird. It's like this bright orange and blue, which are our rival school's colors, so everyone in the staff lounge was complaining about it. The principal said it was cheaper, but you'd think they could at least customize the colors, right? I was trying to figure out how to input a late assignment yesterday, and I ended up clicking on this link that took me to a totally different page about state standards. Which is a whole other thing, because they keep changing the standards every few years, and we have to rewrite all our lesson plans...\n\nWait, sorry. What was the question? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't think I'm a big worrier. Just regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you already have these same attention and forgetfulness problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. I mean, as far back as I can remember, really. Even in primary school, my report cards always said the exact same things, like, \"bright but careless\" or \"doesn't finish work unless closely supervised.\" My parents were constantly finding these crumpled up worksheets stuffed at the bottom of my backpack that I completely forgot to turn in. \n\nWhich, honestly, backpacks these days are so much better designed. Have you seen the ones my students carry? They have all these specific padded compartments for laptops and tablets, and little mesh pockets for water bottles. When I was a kid, we just had those basic canvas ones with one big pocket, and everything just sank to the bottom into this black hole. I had this one that was Ninja Turtles—Donatello, specifically, because he was the smart one with the bo staff. I actually tried to make a bo staff out of a broom handle once in the backyard, and I ended up whacking my mom's prized rose bushes. She was so mad. She used to spend hours on her knees in the garden every Sunday, wearing these huge floppy sun hats... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, the forgetfulness and not finishing things, that's been happening since I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt unusually energetic or driven?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I definitely need my sleep. If I don't get at least seven hours, I'm basically a zombie the next day at school. \n\nAlthough, honestly, getting good sleep has been tough lately because my neighbor just got this new dog. It's some kind of husky mix, I think? And it just howls at all hours of the night. It's not even a bark, it's this long, drawn-out wail, like a wolf. I read somewhere that huskies are super vocal because they were bred to pull sleds and had to communicate with each other over long distances in the snow. Which is fascinating, really. I actually showed my history class this documentary about the Iditarod a few years ago when we were learning about Alaska. Did you know they use these special little booties for the dogs' paws so they don't get cut up on the ice? They're usually bright pink or neon green, which looks so funny against the snow. One of my students actually tried to knit booties for her cat after watching it, but she said the cat just kicked them off immediately and hid under the sofa for two days...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Energy? Yeah, no, I've never had a time where I didn't need sleep and felt super energetic. I'm usually just tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving when you’re supposed to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. I mean, I can sit through a faculty meeting or a movie just fine without feeling like I need to jump out of my chair or pace around or anything. \n\nThough, speaking of movies, I finally went to see that new sci-fi epic everyone's been talking about. The cinematography was incredible, especially the scenes on that desert planet. They actually filmed a lot of it in Jordan, in Wadi Rum, which is the same place they shot Lawrence of Arabia. I've always wanted to visit Jordan. The architecture in Petra is just mind-blowing, how they carved an entire city directly into the rose-red sandstone cliffs. I was telling my students about it when we were covering ancient trade routes, because the Nabataeans who built it got super wealthy from controlling the incense trade. Frankincense and myrrh, mostly. It's crazy to think about how valuable tree resin used to be, right? Like, wars were fought over sap. I actually bought some frankincense essential oil once to see what the big deal was, but it just made my apartment smell like an old church, so I ended up giving it to my mom...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. What was the question? Oh, sitting still. Yeah, no, I don't get restless or feel like I have to keep moving. I'm pretty okay just sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Interrupt? Um, no, I don't think I do that. I mean, I try really hard to be a good listener. It's super important in teaching, obviously. You have to let the kids finish their thoughts, even if it takes them forever to get to the point. \n\nWhich, man, sometimes they really do take forever. Especially the younger ones. I had this one kid, Tyler, a couple years ago, who would raise his hand and then just start telling me about his weekend, completely unrelated to the lesson. Like, we'd be talking about the French Revolution, and suddenly he's giving me a play-by-play of his cousin's birthday party at this trampoline park. Have you ever been to one of those places? They're actually kind of terrifying. It's just wall-to-wall trampolines and foam pits, and kids are just launching themselves everywhere. I went to one for my nephew's birthday last year, and I swear I almost blew out my knee just trying to bounce normally. The foam pits are the worst part, though. You jump in, and it's like quicksand. It took me a solid five minutes to claw my way out, and I felt so ridiculous. Plus, you just know those foam blocks are absolutely covered in germs. I read this article once about how many bacteria they found in a ball pit at a fast-food restaurant, and it was... \n\nWait. Sorry. Interrupting. Right. No, I don't really interrupt people. I usually just let them talk.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and forgetfulness problems cause difficulties for you in more than one setting, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely everywhere. At home, my wife has basically started taking over all the bills and reminders because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. And at work, the missed follow-through has actually become pretty serious. My department chair just put me on a formal support plan. \n\nShe called me into her office to give me the paperwork, and she had it all organized in this blindingly bright yellow folder. Which, honestly, is the worst color for a stressful meeting. It’s that fluorescent highlighter yellow? I read this study once about how different colors affect your mood and productivity. Like, blue is supposed to be super calming and helps you focus, which is why a lot of corporate offices use it. But yellow is actually known to cause anxiety if you're exposed to too much of it. It's why fast-food places use so much yellow and red—it supposedly makes you hungry but also makes you feel rushed so you eat fast and leave. McDonald's, Burger King, they all do it. I tried to explain that color psychology stuff to my wife when we were picking out paint for the kitchen, but she went with this pale yellow anyway. It was called 'Morning Butter.' Which is just a weird name for paint, right? Like, who wants their walls to remind them of dairy? I don't even really eat butter anymore since I've been trying to watch my cholesterol. I switched to that olive oil spread, but it just doesn't melt the same way on a toasted bagel...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm rambling again. What were you asking? Oh, right, difficulties in different settings. But yeah, so, it's definitely causing issues for me both at school with the support plan and at home with my wife having to manage all the household stuff.\n\nDoctor: In school as a child, did you have difficulty with reading, writing, or math itself, or was the problem mainly staying focused and finishing work?\n\nPatient: Reading and math were actually fine for me, I understood the material. It was definitely just staying focused and actually finishing the work. Even back in primary school, my report cards always said things like, \"bright but careless,\" or \"doesn't finish work unless closely supervised.\" And my parents were constantly finding all these half-finished worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. \n\nBackpacks were so different back then, though. I had this really cool Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles one in second grade. It was one of those plastic-y ones that cracked if it got too cold? But it had Donatello on it, he was always my favorite because he was the smart one who invented all their gadgets. Did you know the creators of Ninja Turtles originally just drew them as a joke? Like a parody of all the gritty comic books that were popular in the 80s, like Daredevil and Ronin. It's wild how something created as a total joke became this massive billion-dollar franchise with toys and movies and everything. I actually found some of my old action figures in my parents' attic a few months ago. The plastic gets kind of sticky when it gets old, which is super gross. It's called polymer degradation, I think. The plasticizers they use to make the plastic flexible start to seep out over time. It's a huge problem for museums trying to preserve modern artifacts, like old spacesuits from the Apollo missions. They're literally just melting away in storage because nobody knows how to stop the chemical breakdown...\n\nOh, wait. Sorry. I'm doing it again. Um, but yeah, the school stuff. The subjects themselves weren't the problem, it was just the focusing and actually getting the work done and turned in.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have significant trouble learning to read, write, or do math when you were in school?\n\nPatient: No, no trouble learning them. Like I was just saying, I understood the material fine, it was just the focusing part that was hard. I actually really liked reading when I was a kid, especially those Choose Your Own Adventure books? \n\nMan, I loved those. You know, where it says \"If you want to open the door, turn to page 42. If you want to run away, turn to page 73.\" I used to keep all my fingers tucked into the pages so I could go back and change my choice if I died. Which kind of defeats the purpose of the book, I guess, but I hated getting a bad ending. I think the guy who invented those books was actually a lawyer or something, and he came up with the idea while telling bedtime stories to his daughters. He'd ask them what the characters should do next. It's such a brilliant concept, making the reader part of the narrative. They've kind of tried to do that with video games now, with all these branching storylines and multiple endings, but it's not quite the same feeling as physically flipping the pages. I tried to get some of my students into those interactive fiction games online, like Twine games? But they just don't have the patience for all that text without any graphics. Kids today are so used to high-definition everything, they don't want to use their imagination to picture the scene...\n\nSorry. I'm doing it again. But no, to answer your question, no trouble learning to read or do math. Just the staying on task part."
